## Page 1


DYNAMICAL MEAN-FIELD LIMIT AND REPLICA-SYMMETRIC FREE
ENERGY FOR THE ORTHOGONALLY-INVARIANT SK MODEL
ZHOU FAN, THEODOR MISIAKIEWICZ, LEDA WANG, AND GARRETT G. WEN
Abstract. We study a class of diffusion processes on Rn interacting through a symmetric matrix
X ∈Rn×n. When eigenvectors of X are random and uniformly distributed on the orthogonal group,
we derive a dynamical mean-field limit for the empirical law of sample paths, extending a classical
characterization due to Sompolinsky and Zippelius for X ∼GOE. This limit takes the form of a
generalized Langevin equation with correlated Gaussian noise and memory, whose correlation and
response kernels relate to those of the original diffusion process through a system of convolution
equations depending on X via the free cumulants of its eigenvalue distribution.
Specializing to the overdamped Langevin diffusion {θt}t≥0 for a Gibbs measure
µ(θ) ∝exp
1
2θ⊤Xθ

n
Y
i=1
ν(dθi),
we analyze this mean-field limit under an assumption that the dynamics are rapidly mixing. In
this regime, the correlation/response kernels admit time-translation-invariant approximants which
satisfy a fluctuation-dissipation relation. The generalized Langevin equation admits a Markovian
approximation with coupling to an auxiliary multivariate OU-process, and converges in time to a
replica-symmetric prediction for the empirical law of coordinates under µ. The correlation struc-
ture of the auxiliary process may be understood through the infinitesimal generator of a Markov
semigroup for the lifted process of path histories ξt = {θs}s:s≤t.
Consequently, we show that the free energy of the Gibbs measure converges to a replica-
symmetric limit under an explicit high-temperature condition, which for an Ising model is
∥X∥op < 1/2.
By recent dynamical universality results, this implies that the same characterization of the free
energy holds in fully deterministic models without random disorder, as long as X satisfies a set of
deterministic delocalization conditions.

Let X ∈Rn×n be a (deterministic or random) symmetric matrix. Consider a diffusion process
or gradient flow in Rn interacting through X,

For models where X has independent Gaussian entries up to transposition symmetry, such ques-
tions are richly studied in the physics and mathematics literatures on spin glasses and disordered
systems. A dynamical mean-field limit for a prototypical example of (1.1) was first derived by Som-
polinsky and Zippelius in [62], and is given by the law of a scalar generalized Langevin equation

(Cθ, Rθ). Convergence in empirical law of the sample paths of (1.1) to (1.3), and an associated
annealed large deviations principle, was first proved mathematically by Ben Arous and Guionnet
in [2, 39]. The Ising case of the Gibbs measure (1.2) with ν(dθ) supported on {±1} corresponds to
the classical Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (S-K) model [61], for which the form of the asymptotic free
energy was famously conjectured by Parisi in [57] and proved by Guerra and Talagrand [38, 64];
we refer to [65, 66, 56] for textbook treatments of this model and its rich history. Universality over
classes of random matrices X having independent non-Gaussian entries (up to symmetry) has been
shown for the free energy of the S-K model in [11] and for dynamics similar to (1.1) in [17, 19].
Arguably, this universality class of disorder matrices with independent entries remains quite lim-
ited, raising a question of whether analogous results hold under more general mean-field structure.
In this work, we aim to extend some of these developments to a class of disorder matrices X ∈Rn×n
that are orthogonally-invariant in law — i.e. whose matrix of eigenvectors is uniformly distributed
on the orthogonal group — and to an associated universality class that encompasses examples of
deterministic matrices X. Our contributions are threefold:
1. For the dynamics (1.1) with orthogonally-invariant X ∈Rn×n having a compactly supported
asymptotic spectral law Λ, we develop an extension of the mean-field limit (1.3).
This extension has the same structural form as (1.3),

(In the setting of the classical S-K model where X = βJ and J ∼GOE(n), this corresponds to
the assumption β < 1/4, which is a subset of the full replica-symmetric regime [47].) Leveraging
the dynamical universality results of [25, 68], we deduce that the same conclusion holds when
h = 0 for a general class of purely deterministic matrices X ∈Rn×n satisfying delocalization
conditions (cf. Assumption 2.9), and for arbitrary h ∈R when X is a “semi-random” matrix
conjugated by a diagonal matrix of random {±1} signs.
These results are presented in Section 2.
1.1. Existing literature. Dynamics. The form of the mean-field limit (1.4) for the diffusion equa-
tion (1.1) with orthogonally-invariant X may be anticipated from the analysis of Opper, C¸akmak,
and Winther [53], who computed the dynamical mean-field equations for an analogous class of
discrete-time dynamics. The mean-field limit (1.4) and convolutional structure of its correlation
and response kernels may be understood as a passage of this result to continuous time.
A particular class of discrete-time dynamics — analogous to Approximate Message Passing
(AMP) algorithms [42, 24] and TAP iterations [8] in models with Gaussian disorder X — was
highlighted in [53] as having purely Gaussian limiting dynamics. For this AMP class, convergence
to the mean-field limit was proved mathematically in [29], extending an approach of iterative condi-
tioning of Haar-orthogonal matrices previously developed in [48, 63, 59] to analyze a simpler family
of divergence-free “vector-AMP” algorithms. Alternative proofs and extensions of the result of [29]
have since been obtained in [46, 37], and universality over semi-random and deterministic disorder
matrices X has been shown in increasingly general contexts in [25, 68, 37].
It was shown by Celentano, Cheng, and Montanari [12], in an analogous statistical model with
i.i.d. Gaussian disorder, that the mean-field limit of general discrete-time iterative algorithms may
be deduced from that of AMP, and also that the mean-field limit of continuous-time gradient flow
dynamics may be proved using an approach different from [2, 39]. This approach relies on a reduc-
tion to AMP dynamics via time-discretization, and a passage to the continuous-time limit through


## Page 4


4
DMFT FOR THE ORTHOGONALLY-INVARIANT SK MODEL
verifying contractivity of the dynamical fixed-point equations in a suitably defined Banach metric.
The approach has been extended to derive mean-field limits for broad families of discrete-time
iterative algorithms in [36, 40, 13, 15] and for continuous-time Langevin diffusions and stochastic
gradient dynamics in [30, 33, 52].
We apply a similar proof strategy in our work, to pass the
characterization of AMP in [29] to continuous time.
Mathematical analysis of aging in the dynamical mean-field limit of a spherical 2-spin model was
carried out in [1], of convergence in time of the Cugliandolo-Kurchan/Crisanti-Horner-Sommers
equations of the spherical p-spin model to an FDT equilibrium in [18], and of convergence in time
of these equations under disorder-dependent initial conditions in [20, 21]. An analysis of the dy-
namical mean-field limit for a non-spherical model with Gaussian disorder was carried out in [31]
under a convergence assumption similar to our work; we build upon several ideas of [31], discussed
in Section 1.2.
Free energy. The Ising case of the model (1.2) was introduced by Marinari, Parisi, and Ritort
[50] as a model which may exhibit similar mean-field phenomena as models with deterministic
disorder. Replica-symmetric and 1-RSB predictions for the asymptotic free energy of this model
were computed in [50, 14]. TAP equations for the magnetization were derived in [58] using a high-
temperature expansion and in [54] using an alternative cavity method. [49] extended the former
analysis to a broad class of statistical models with orthogonally-invariant random disorder, and
discussed its connection to dynamical algorithms.
Mathematical proofs of such predictions remain scarce in the literature, even in high temperature.
For a high-temperature regime of the Ising model without external field, where quenched and
annealed free energies coincide, a replica-symmetric limit for the free energy was proved in [7] using
the second-moment method. This was extended to an Ising model with external field h ∈R in [34]
via a conditional second-moment method analogous to that of [9] for the S-K model and [22] for the
Ising perceptron, conditioning on the filtration of a TAP iteration for computing the magnetization.
This result of [34] showed, for X = βJ where the asymptotic spectral law of J converges to ¯Λ, that
the free energy converges to its replica-symmetric prediction under a high-temperature condition
of the form β ∈(0, β0) for some
β0 ≡β0(Law(¯Λ), h).
As the analysis of the second-moment variational problem in [34] was perturbative around β = 0,
it is difficult to glean a more explicit bound for β0, and we believe that this bound would be
substantially smaller than our explicit guarantee in (1.7). For an analogous statistical model where
the disorder matrix X is alternatively representable using Gaussian rather than Haar-orthogonal
randomness, the asymptotic free energy was proved in [4] via a Gaussian interpolation approach. A
replica-symmetric limit for the ground state energy of a Gibbs measure with orthogonally-invariant
X and convex potential was also proved in [35] via analysis of a vector-AMP algorithm for convex
optimization.
One perspective on the challenge of using a dynamical approach to prove properties of the Gibbs
measure is in certifying the optimality of the dynamics.
For example, in the context of (1.2)
and related statistical models, AMP algorithms/TAP iterations that conjecturally converge to the
magnetization are known [53, 59, 10], but this convergence has thus far only been certified in high-
temperature regimes analogous to that of [34], relying on a-priori understanding of the asymptotic
free energy and structure of the Gibbs measure [32, 45]. Likewise, while mean-field characterizations
of the ground state energy in convex landscapes may be established by certifying optimality of
optimization algorithms through a first-order condition [6, 23, 35], certifying global optimality of
such algorithms in non-convex landscapes remains challenging. In this work, we take an approach
of analyzing the standard overdamped Langevin dynamics, which has a more complex dynamical
mean-field limit than AMP-type algorithms, but whose convergence to the Gibbs measure may be
certified through entropy-decay arguments external to the mean-field theory.

DMFT FOR THE ORTHOGONALLY-INVARIANT SK MODEL
5
As a benefit of our dynamical approach to analyzing the free energy, our results inherit the
aforementioned universality over semi-random and deterministic matrices X [25, 68, 37], which has
been shown for algorithm dynamics but not yet for static properties of the Gibbs measure. Our
results confirm that spin-glass/disordered-systems characterizations of the Gibbs measure can hold
for systems without random disorder, as originally anticipated by [50].
1.2. Proof ideas. We describe briefly the main ideas of our analyses.
As discussed in the preceding section, (1.4) is obtained by passing a characterization of a discrete-
time AMP algorithm in [53, 29] to continuous time.
An important distinction between these
discrete-time and continuous-time characterizations is that the correlation/response kernels of the
former are defined iteratively/inductively over time. The first contribution of our work is to for-
malize a sense in which the continuous-time kernels Cθ, Rθ, Cg, Rg may instead be understood as
the fixed points of a contractive map.
An obstacle to this formalization is that the convolutional equations mapping
(Cθ, Rθ) 7→(Cg, Rg)
(1.8)
do not, in general, ensure that Cg remains a positive-semidefinite (p.s.d.) kernel, which is needed to
define the process (1.4); thus, it is a bit unclear on what domain such a contractive map should be
defined. A related issue is that even when Cg is p.s.d., it seems challenging to analyze contractivity
of (1.8) under a Wasserstein-coupling metric for Cθ, Cg as used in the arguments of [12, 30, 33].
We resolve these issues by formalizing the fixed-point interpretation of the dynamical mean-field
limit via a sequence of mappings
(Cθ, Rθ) 7→(Cg, Rg) 7→P(Cg, Rg) 7→(Cθ, Rθ),
where P projects Cg under an alternative weighted L2 metric onto a subset of the p.s.d. cone. We
then develop different arguments to establish contractivity in this metric, and leverage results of
[29] to show that at the fixed point of these mappings, Cg is in fact p.s.d., and thus is also a fixed
point without projection by P.
Turning to the analysis of the overdamped Langevin equation, from the assumed convergence
condition in Assumption 2.5 and an identification of (Cθ, Rθ) as the limits of coordinate-wise corre-
lation and response functions of {θt}t≥0, one may deduce the existence of time-translation-invariant
limits cθ, rθ for these kernels at large times, together with their fluctuation-dissipation relation
rθ(t) = −c′
θ(t)
(1.9)
(Lemma 4.1). These arguments are similar to [31] which analyzed a statistical model with Gaussian
disorder.
Interestingly, we are able to verify that (1.9) implies also the fluctuation-dissipation
relation rg(t) = −c′
g(t) corresponding to the kernels Cg, Rg appearing in the generalized Langevin
equation (1.4). This verification is a short calculation in the Fourier domain (Lemma 4.2).
We show that cg(·) may be interpreted as the large-n limit of the correlation function of coordi-
nates of

L2(ν). Thus, we do not obtain from this representation a mixture-of-exponentials form for cg(·),
and in general, we do not expect such a form to hold true. Instead, we establish an approximation
cg(t) ≈u⊤

where −Am ∈Rm×m is a (non-diagonal, asymmetric) matrix that constitutes a finite-dimensional
approximation for the generator of {Pt}t≥0. Together with the fluctuation-dissipation relation (1.5),
this leads to the previously stated Markov approximation (1.6) for (1.4).
Finally, we show convergence of (1.4) to an equilibrium law characterized by replica-symmetric
fixed point equations, by analyzing convergence of the Markovian system (1.6) together with its
approximation error for (1.4). The primary technical challenge is that the generator −A for the
above semigroup {Pt}t≥0 has non-trivial (in fact, dense) null space in L2(ν), and thus the spectral
gap of its approximant −Am must vanish with the approximation error. Thus, it seems challeng-
ing to prove convergence to equilibrium of the Markovian system (1.6) over a fixed time horizon
that is independent of this error and the dimension m; on the other hand, the accumulated ap-
proximation error over a m-dependent time horizon may not be small under a naive coupling and
Gr¨onwall argument. We circumvent this issue by using a careful construction of −Am that instead
approximates a slightly damped version of −A, and that allows for a stable coupling of (1.4) and
(1.6) over an arbitrarily long time horizon on which (1.6) is guaranteed to converge (Corollary 5.2,
Lemma 5.3). This implies the replica-symmetric characterization of the Gibbs measure (1.2) under
Assumption 2.5, for laws ν having a smooth potential. The concrete implication for the Ising model
is then deduced by verifying Assumption 2.5 using the log-Sobolev inequality of [5] for a sequence
of double-well potentials that converge to the Ising law.

This limit (2.22) coincides with the replica formula first computed in [50], and proved previously

2.3. Universality over the disorder. Orthogonal invariance of the disorder matrix X in As-
sumption 2.1 underlies a mean-field characterization of a discrete-time AMP algorithm in [29],
from which the dynamical mean-field limit in Theorem 2.4 and results for the Gibbs measure in
Theorem 2.6 and Corollaries 2.7 and 2.8 are derived.
It has been shown, in increasing generality in [25, 68, 37], that this characterization holds more
broadly over universality classes of (possibly deterministic) matrices X ∈Rn×n. This implies, as a
consequence, that our preceding results carry over to such universality classes.
We provide here a concrete statement of universality for one such class of deterministic disorder
matrices X studied in [25, 68].

that as n →∞, the empirical eigenvalue distribution of X satisfies

Corollary 2.10. Suppose X satisfies Assumption 2.9.
(a) If Assumption 2.2 holds, f : R →R is odd, and θ0 is sign invariant in law (i.e. f(x) = −f(−x)
for all x ∈R, and Law(θ0) = Law(−θ0)), then Theorem 2.4 holds for the dynamics {θt}t≥0.
(b) If U(·) and Λ satisfy all conditions of Corollary 2.7, and U : R →R is even (i.e. U(x) = U(−x)
for all x ∈R), then all statements of Theorem 2.6 hold for µGibbs.
(c) If h = 0 in the Ising model (2.21) and Λmax < 1/2, then all statements of Corollary 2.8 hold.
(d) If f(·) is not odd, θ0 is not sign-invariant in law, U(·) is not even, or h ̸= 0, then the same
conclusions hold for the dynamics and Gibbs measures defined with SXS in place of X, where
S is a diagonal matrix of i.i.d. Rademacher ±1 signs independent of θ0 and {bt}t≥0.
Remark 2.11. Let X = (Id −1

n11⊤) be a “puncturing” of a deterministic matrix
H satisfying the conditions of Assumption 2.9, and let ˜X = (Id −1

spectral limit Law(Λ). One may also verify using [68, Proposition 2.7, Lemma B.1] and the recent
result of [37, Theorem 5.3] that, even if f(·) is not odd / U(·) is not even, the mean-field limits for
the dynamics {θt}t≥0 and Gibbs measure µGibbs coincide for X and ˜X.
However, without further assumptions for f(·) / U(·) such as the sign symmetry conditions in
Corollary 2.10 above, these limits would, in general, be different from those stated in Theorems 2.4
and 2.6 for the unpunctured orthogonally-invariant matrix ˜H. It is possible to derive analogues of
our results for this puncturing of ˜H, but for brevity we will not do so in the current work.

The remainder of this paper proves the preceding results.
Section 3 proves Theorem 2.4 on
the dynamical mean-field limit, Section 4 analyzes the kernels Cθ, Rθ, Cg, Rg for the overdamped
Langevin diffusion at equilibrium, Section 5 proves Theorem 2.6 and Corollaries 2.7 and 2.8 on the
Gibbs measure, and Section 6 proves Corollary 2.10 on universality over the disorder.
3. Dynamical mean-field limit
In this section, we prove Theorem 2.4. We will omit arguments that are similar to ones in [30]
for brevity, and focus on the differences in our setting of a non-Gaussian disorder matrix X.

4. Analyses of correlation and response
We proceed to prove Theorem 2.6 on the replica-symmetric limit for the overlaps and free energy
of the Gibbs measure µGibbs, by specializing the preceding dynamical mean-field limit to the over-
damped Langevin equation (2.11). Our analysis relies on several important structural properties of
the kernels Cθ, Rθ, Cg, Rg at large times. We collect these properties in the following two lemmas.
First, under Assumption 2.5, we have the following properties for Cθ, Rθ, including the existence
of time-translation-invariant limits cθ, rθ : R →R for these kernels as t →∞, and a fluctuation-
dissipation relation between these limits.

5. Replica-symmetric overlaps and free energy
In this section, we now prove Theorem 2.6 and Corollaries 2.7 and 2.8 on the asymptotic overlaps
and free energy of the Gibbs measure (2.8).
The argument is to establish a Markovian approximation of the generalized Langevin equation
(2.2), using the fluctuation-dissipation relation between cg, rg in Lemma 4.2, an approximation of
c0
g(τ) = cg(τ) −lims→∞cg(s) by the correlation function of observables of the lifted Markovian

process ξt = {θs}s∈[t−B,t] over path histories, a further approximation of this correlation function
by a kernel ˜cg defined via a finite-dimensional approximation of its infinitesimal generator, and a
stable coupling between the dynamics driven by c0
g and ˜cg.
The approximation of c0
g (and its derivative −rg) is carried out in the following lemma.

6. Universality over the disorder
We now prove Corollary 2.10 on universality over the disorder X, using the results of [68].
Proof of Corollary 2.10. Let ˜X ∈Rn×n satisfy Assumption 2.9. Let X ∈Rn×n be an orthogonally-
invariant matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1 with the same bound Λmax and asymptotic eigenvalue
distribution Λ as ˜X. Let S ∈Rn×n be a random diagonal sign matrix as in Corollary 2.10(d), and
let Π ∈Rn×n be a uniformly random permutation matrix, where (S, Π) are independent of each
other and of θ0, {bt}t≥0. Then [68, Proposition 2.7, Theorem 2.8] implies that the AMP algorithm
(3.35) applied with ˜X has the same dynamical mean-field limit (3.37) (in the sense of almost-sure
Wasserstein-2 convergence of the coordinatewise empirical distribution of its iterates) as applied
with the matrix
W = ΠS ˜XSΠ⊤.
As (3.37) and the bound ∥X∥op ≤Λmax are the only properties of X used in all preceding proofs,
this implies that Theorems 2.4, 2.6 and Corollaries 2.7, 2.8 hold equally with W in place of X.
The dynamics (2.1) driven by W have the form
dθt = [f(θt) + ΠS ˜XSΠ⊤θt]dt +

Appendix A. Free cumulant bound
Proposition A.1. Suppose Λ is a random variable with |Λ| ≤K for a constant K > 0. Let
{κp}p≥1 be the free cumulants of Λ. Then
lim sup
p→∞|κp|1/p ≤2K.
Proof. By rescaling Λ, assume without loss of generality K = 1. For z ∈C outside the support of
Λ, define the Cauchy transform GΛ(z) = E[1/(z −Λ)]. It is shown in [51, Theorem 17] that GΛ is
univalent over {z : |z| > 4}, that {w : |w| ∈(0, 1/6)} ⊂GΛ({z : |z| > 4}), and that RΛ(w) defined
by (2.13) is absolutely convergent for |w| < 1/6 and given by
RΛ(w) = G−1
Λ (w) −1
w for |w| ∈(0, 1/6)
(A.1)
where G−1
Λ (w) denotes the unique inverse in {z : |z| > 4}.
We check that RΛ extends analytically to |w| < 1/2: Define, on the open disk D = {z : |z| < 1},
the analytic function
H(z) = GΛ(1/z) = E[z/(1 −zΛ)].
For any z ∈D and |Λ| ≤1, note that |1 −zΛ|2 ≤1 + Λ2 −2ℜ[z]Λ ≤(2 + ε)(1 −ℜ[z]Λ). Then
ℜ[1/(1 −zΛ)] = (1 −ℜ[z]Λ)/|1 −zΛ|2 ≥1/(2 + ε). This implies E[1/(1 −zΛ)] ̸= 0, so z = 0 is the
unique simple root of H(z) on D. Furthermore,
|H(z)| ≥|z| · ℜE[1/(1 −zΛ)] ≥1 −ε
2 + ε for |z| = 1 −ε.
Then by Rouch´e’s theorem, for each w ∈C with |w| < (1 −ε)/(2 + ε), H(z) = w also has a unique
simple root z in the disk {z : |z| < 1−ε}. Since H′(z) ̸= 0 at this root, the inverse function theorem
implies that there exists an analytic inverse H−1 on {w : |w| < (1 −ε)/(2 + ε)}. Since H−1(0) = 0,
we have |H−1(w)| < 1/4 on some neighborhood O of w = 0, and GΛ(1/H−1(w)) = H(H−1(w)) = w.
Then the above univalence implies 1/H−1(w) = G−1
Λ (w) for all w ∈O. Then the right side of (A.1)
has an analytic extension to {w : |w| ∈(0, (1 −ε)/(2 + ε))}. Since ε > 0 is arbitrary, this implies
RΛ(w) extends analytically to |w| < 1/2, as claimed. Then RΛ(w) is given by the convergent series
(2.13) for all |w| < 1/2, so the root test implies that lim supp→∞|κp|1/p ≤2.
□
